GRAPHIC ANALYSIS OF THE CONSTRUCTIONAL EVOLUTION OF THE
CARMONA ALCÁZAR WITH AN HBIM MODEL

Inmaculada León Fernández  a1, Manuel Castellano Román  a2*
a Architectural Graphic Expression Department, University of Seville, Spain.
a1 inmaleonfernandez@gmail.com, a2 manuelcr@us.es

Abstract
The Alcázar de Arriba of Carmona is a 10th-century fortress of Islamic origin that underwent continuous
alterations in subsequent periods, reaching its greatest splendour during the Late Medieval Christian
period under the reign of Peter I. At present, it survives in a state of ruin as a result of its historical
development; however, thanks to the analysis of archaeological interventions carried out at the site and
to the geometric documentation produced by other researchers, it is possible to propose a hypothesis
regarding its original configuration and its constructional evolution. The objective of this study is to
provide a diachronic graphic interpretation of this evolution through the generation of an HBIM model
capable of integrating the available knowledge about the building and graphically grading the level of
uncertainty associated with the proposed hypotheses.

Keywords: Architectural Heritage; Defensive Architecture; Architectural Analysis; HBIM.

Resumen
El Alcázar de Arriba de Carmona, también conocido como Alcázar del Rey Don Pedro, es una fortaleza
con orígenes musulmanes del siglo X, que fue sometido a continuas reformas en las etapas posteriores,
alcanzado su máximo esplendor en la época bajomedieval cristiana durante el reinado de Pedro I.
Actualmente se encuentra en un estado de ruina fruto de su devenir histórico, pero gracias a los
análisis de las intervenciones arqueológicas realizadas y a los trabajos de documentación geométrica
desarrollados por otros investigadores, se puede plantear una hipótesis de su estado original y su
evolución constructiva, lo que constituye el objetivo de la investigación. Para ello se lleva a cabo la
elaboración de un modelo HBIM del conjunto, que ha permitido ofrecer una lectura gráfica de su
evolución constructiva.

Palabras clave: Patrimonio arquitectónico; Arquitectura Defensiva; Análisis Arquitectónico; HBIM.

5.6 FINAL PHASE (17TH–21ST CENTURIES)
﻿
Decline (17th–20th Centuries)
For more than four centuries, the events
associated with the Alcázar did not lead to
significant developments; on the contrary, they
clearly illustrate its progressive decline. Its role
as a defensive fortress gradually lost relevance,
while its function as a royal residence also
diminished over time.
Throughout these centuries, a series of events
contributed to the progressive deterioration
of the complex. In the 17th century, it was used
as a hospital and cemetery during outbreaks of
plague; in 1755, the Lisbon earthquake caused
severe damage to the structure; in 1868, it served
as a gathering point for agricultural labourers
during the revolution known as La Gloriosa; in Fig. 16. Entrance to a cave excavated on the north side
1873, troops were quartered in the fortress during of the moat, used as a dwelling in the 20th century.
the First Spanish Republic, in connection with the Source: the authors.

LEÓN FERNÁNDEZ, I. AND CASTELLANO ROMÁN, M.

century, the enclosure also contained agricultural ﻿
gardens; and, on the occasion of the 1982 FIFA
World Cup, a football pitch was installed within
the complex.

Revival (20th–21st Centuries)
It was also during the 20th century that the Alcázar
began to attract renewed heritage interest, and
the historical, artistic and cultural value of the
monument started to be more widely recognised.
The first step occurred in 1931, when the
complex was declared a Bien de Interés Cultural Fig. 17. Aereal view of the Alcázar currently. PDA (2011)
(BIC, Good Of Cultural Interest). Paradoxically,
however, several of the events that took place

ANÁLISIS GRÁFICO DE LA EVOLUCIÓN CONSTRUCTIVA DEL ALCÁZAR DE CARMONA MEDIANTE MODELO HBIM

\6. CONCLUSIONES por haberme facilitado todos los archivos utilizados en
la redacción del Plan Director.
Llegados a este punto de la investigación, se pueden
plantear conclusiones en base a tres aspectos: la
propuesta de evolución constructiva, las aportaciones FINANCIACIÓN
de la generación del modelo HBIM y las perspectivas Este artículo ha contado con la financiación del VIIde uso del modelo para la tutela del bien. Plan Propio de Investigación del Vicerrectorado de
En relación con la propuesta de la evolución Investigación de la Universidad de Sevilla, a través del
constructiva del Alcázar se ha elaborado una síntesis departamento de Expresión Gráfica Arquitectónica de
del conocimiento disponible que se ha formalizado en la ETSA de Sevilla.”
imágenes capaces de validar las hipótesis planteadas
y de generar otras nuevas (Fig. 19).
En cuanto a la metodología HBIM utilizada para el
análisis del objeto de estudio, es importante entender
el valor de estos recursos y procesos, que han
permitido la visualización tridimensional del conjunto,
ayudando a la compresión del espacio, y al mismo
tiempo permitiendo la identificación y caracterización
de sus elementos mediante parámetros tales como
su fase de creación y derribo, su materialidad o su
grado de certeza. La generación del modelo es una
forma de procesar y asumir la información recopilada
y analizada, y permite al mismo tiempo hacerse más
preguntas y buscar respuestas que, de no haber sido
por su intento de reconstrucción virtual, probablemente
no se hubieran planteado.
Al haber optado por la realización de un modelo
HBIM, ahora se cuenta con una base volumétrica y
de datos, fruto del análisis que se ha hecho sobre el
inmueble. Esto permite gestionar la información del
edificio patrimonial, documentando su estructura y
arquitectura y facilitando la comprensión de su historia
y evolución que eran los objetivos que se perseguían
con este trabajo.
Finalmente, el modelo constituye una construcción
abierta, es decir, susceptible de su desarrollo
futuro mediante el refinamiento progresivo de su
información, tanto geométrica como alfanumérica. El
modelo generado permite hasta este punto identificar
diacrónicamente las fases constructivas del conjunto,
sintetizar el conocimiento disponible y servir de
asistente para la generación de nuevo conocimiento y
servir como soporte gráfico para acciones de difusión,
dinamización y puesta en valor. Pero ulteriores
desarrollos, además de profundizar en esos usos,
permitirían asistir en la planificación estratégica
del conjunto o en las acciones de conservación e
intervención material sobre éste.

HBIM DIGITAL METHODOLOGIES IN HERITAGE CONSERVATION: A SYSTEMATIC
REVIEW OF RELIGIOUS AND DEFENSIVE ARCHITECTURE (2010–2025)

Jonathan Moreno-Collado  a, M. Lourdes Gutiérrez-Carrillo  b
a Department of Art History, University of Granada, Spain. morenohuelma@correo.ugr.es.
b Department of Architectural Constructions, University of Granada, Spain. mlgutier@ugr.es

Abstract
The development of digital methodologies has significantly expanded the use of HBIM for documenting
and conserving architectural heritage. This study examines HBIM applications in religious and
defensive architecture through a systematic literature review covering the period 2010–2025. Following
the PRISMA protocol, searches were conducted in Scopus, Web of Science, and IEEE Xplore, initially
identifying 540 records, of which 61 studies met the inclusion criteria. The analysis considers data
acquisition strategies, modelling environments, Levels of Development, and conservation-oriented
applications. Results indicate the predominance of hybrid survey methodologies, particularly the
integration of terrestrial laser scanning and UAV photogrammetry. However, a persistent imbalance
between geometric modelling (LOG) and semantic information (LOI) limits the operational use of HBIM
models in long-term conservation management. The review also reveals a strong typological bias
toward religious architecture, highlighting the need to expand HBIM research to territorially complex
defensive heritage.

Keywords: HBIM; scan-to-BIM; religious heritage; defensive heritage; systematic literature review; LOD.

Resumen
El avance de las metodologías digitales ha impulsado el uso de HBIM en la documentación y conservación
del patrimonio arquitectónico. Este estudio analiza su aplicación en arquitectura religiosa y defensiva
mediante una revisión sistemática de la literatura (2010–2025). Siguiendo el protocolo PRISMA, se
consultaron las bases de datos Scopus, Web of Science e IEEE Xplore, identificándose inicialmente
540 registros, cumpliendo 61 estudios con los criterios de inclusión. El análisis aborda estrategias de
captura tridimensional, entornos de modelado, niveles de desarrollo y aplicaciones en conservación.
Los resultados muestran el predominio de metodologías híbridas de levantamiento, especialmente la
combinación de escáner láser terrestre y fotogrametría UAV. Sin embargo, persiste un desequilibrio

INNOVATION FOR LEARNING IN THE ARCHITECTURE DEGREE:
AN EXPERIENCE IN DRAWING 1

Patricia Ferreira-Lopes  a1*, Roque Angulo Fornos  a2
a Departamento de Expresión Gráfica Arquitectónica/ Instituto Universitario de Arquitectura y Ciencias de la
Construcción, Universidad de Sevilla, España. a1 pwanderley@us.es, a2 roqueaf@us.es.

Abstract
University teaching should be oriented towards the continuous improvement of student learning. In
this regard, addressing students’ spontaneous ideas and identifying their prior knowledge are highly
significant for enhancing teaching practice. This study presents the implementation of a methodology
based on the Improvement Cycles in Classroom (ICIC) in the course Drawing 1: Geometry and Perception
within the Bachelor’s Degree in Fundamentals of Architecture. Specifically, it reports its application to
the topic “Surfaces and Volumes”. To this end, contrast activities, practical exercises, and observation-
based tasks involving architectural elements were designed, all aimed at their geometric documentation
through the dihedral and axonometric systems. The evaluation of this proposal reveals a positive impact
derived from the application of this methodology, which enables the objective identification of students’
learning progress.

Keywords: architectural graphic expression; geometry and perception; teaching innovation; graphic
representation; graphic language.

Resumen
La enseñanza universitaria debe orientarse hacia una mejora continua del aprendizaje del alumnado.
En este sentido, abordar las ideas espontáneas del alumnado y conocer sus conocimientos previos
resultan de gran significado para la mejora de la enseñanza. Este estudio expone la implementación de
la metodología basada en el Ciclo de Mejora en el Aula (CIMA) en la asignatura Dibujo 1: Geometría
y Percepción del Grado en Fundamentos de la Arquitectura. En concreto, se presenta su aplicación
en el tema “Superficies y Volúmenes”. Para ello, se diseñaron actividades de contraste, ejercicios
prácticos y dinámicas de observación de elementos arquitectónicos, orientadas a su documentación
geométrica mediante los sistemas diédrico y axonométrico. La evaluación de esa propuesta revela un
València impactoaprendizajepositivode maneraderivadoobjetiva.de la aplicación de esta metodología que permite identificar un progreso de
de

GEOMETRIC AND CONSTRUCTIVE SINGULARITIES IN THE STRUCTURAL
TRANSITION SOLUTIONS IN VANDELVIRA’S WORK: LA GUARDIA AS A CASE STUDY

Antonio Estepa Rubio a*, Jesús Estepa Rubio b
a Escuela de Arquitectura y Tecnología, Universidad San Jorge, Spain. aestepa@usj.es
b Investigador independiente, ER arquitectos, Spain. oficina@erarquitectos.com

Abstract
This article explores the plastic, technical, and executive conditions devised by Vandelvira to resolve
certain structural transitions in his architectural projects, particularly when addressing the challenges
posed by the mechanical unloading of airborne elements with curvilinear layouts onto straight or
polygonal walls. The formal analysis of the solution constructed by Andrés de Vandelvira in the chapel
of La Guardia, together with the study of the theoretical developments drawn by his son Alonso based
on the same architectural model, provides insight into the overall strategy and technical expertise
employed by the architect from Alcaraz to tackle these structural, compositional, and constructive
conflicts. Furthermore, by delving into these considerations, we also affirm the profound value of the
differentiation and singularity of the architectural advancements developed in the southern Iberian
Peninsula during the 16th century, particularly in relation to the duality of intent and construction that
underlies this architectural tradition.

Keywords: Vandelvira; estereotomía; superficies; geometría; construcción.

Resumen
En este artículo reflexionamos sobre las condiciones plásticas, técnicas y ejecutivas que Vandelvira
ideó para resolver algunas de las transiciones estructurales planteadas en sus proyectos, sobre todo,
cuando tuvo que dar respuesta a los conflictos derivados de la descarga mecánica de piezas aéreas
con trazados curvilíneos sobre paramentos rectos o poligonales. El análisis formal de la propuesta
construída por Andrés de Vandelvira en la capilla del convento de La Guardia, junto con el estudio de los
desarrollos teóricos que fueron dibujados por su hijo Alonso sobre ese mismo modelo arquitectónico,
sirven para comprender la estrategia global y la pericia técnica empleada por el arquitecto de Alcaraz
para atajar esos conflictos estructurales, compositivos y constructivos. Además, profundizando en estas
València consideraciones,los avances desarrolladosconseguimosen eltambiénsur peninsularcertificara loel largohondodelvalorsiglodexvila diferenciaciónen relación cony lasingularidadambivalenciade
de propositiva y constructiva que subyace en esta arquitectura.
Politècnica Palabras clave: Vandelvira; stereotomy; surfaces; geometry; construction.

this end, special attention is paid to the use
of highly compelling— and at times somewhat
intricate— stereotomic solutions in which
technical, aesthetic, and ornamental decisions
converge. Moreover, these hybrid solutions,
both structural and compositional, reveal an
interaction between theory and practice that
challenges conventional narratives regarding
Renaissance architectural production.

In this set, the study of the Murcia vault— reinterpreted as a succession of equal parts alongand, above all, the Murcia ribbed vault (bóveda a straight angle (180º) which, in the present case,de Murcia por cruceros), acquires particular is decomposed into seven (Estepa & Estepa,relevance, since these are essentially drawn 2016, 237).solutions that reflect upon the work executed

Quijano’s vault, as in the case of the octagonal
apse (ochavo) at La Guardia, may be defined
as a piece generated from the revolution of a
curve contained in a horizontal plane about an
axis which—while perpendicular to the vertical
plane—also lies within the plane of the curve
(Figure 7). Put differently, it constitutes a toroidal
surface of revolution which, as with spherical
surfaces (Castaño, 2013, 42–44), must be
executed using stereotomic roughing templates
arranged in courses parallel to the planes by
which the surface is sectioned.

Fig. 8. Military axonometric view of the Murcia ribbed
vault. Source: Author’s own graphic material.

The procedures used in Alonso de Vandelvira’s
stonecutting treatise to address the Murcia vault
and the Murcia ribbed vault are not the same. In
the first case, a stereotomic development method
planned from the plan is adopted, whereas in the
second, by contrast, the solution is set out from a
section drawing, in the same way that the round
chapel with ribs (capilla redonda por cruceros)
is resolved. This shows that Vandelvira does
not necessarily bind the constructive solution to
the geometric condition of the intended surface
(Figure 9), as might be assumed at first sight;
rather, he employs control mechanisms for
the stone-cutting pattern adapted to the most
sensible and coherent definition of the ashlar
Fig. 7. Geometric layout of the Ochavo at La Guardia. pieces to be carved and, naturally, to their most
Source: Author’s own graphic material. effective on-site assembly.

joints are placed in zones where structural stress
is, in principle, less detrimental.
On the basis of a comparative reading of Alonso
de Vandelvira’s drawings (Cruz, 2001, 48–59), we
may determine that, conceptually, the octagonal
apse at La Guardia (Estepa, 2017, 153–160) could
be regarded as a particular case of the Murcia
ribbed vault, even though recent studies have
shown its nature to be closer to spherical than
toroidal (Salcedo et al., 2021, 1–9). Nevertheless,
as occurs in many of his works, in this piece the
master from Alcaraz makes use of recognizable
resources that “seal” the proposal with the stamp
of his authorship, thereby differentiating this
project, quite naturally, from the Murcian case.
To resolve this chevet head, Vandelvira devised
an interesting and distinct structural transition
solution with respect to the other octagonal
perimeters that appear in his son Alonso’s
stonecutting treatise. It consists of inserting
diagonal relieving arches in plan in order to ensure
a greater length of contact and, consequently, an
appropriate mechanical transition between the
coffered vault and the wall plane. Thus, although
the nave plan is rectangular at ground contact,
the linear development of the entablature is
defined by a half-octagon. This not only enables
a simpler occupation of the space, but also allows
compositional strata to be distinguished with their
own plastic, aesthetic, and material autonomy
behind the altar.
The two diagonal traces of the octagonal
perimeter are, in turn, subdivided into another
pair of elements that are equally legible within
the composition: semicircular arches appear
above and, beneath them, scallop-shell squinches
(trompas aveneradas). The arches serve to
prevent the linear entablature from remaining

GEOMETRIC AND CONSTRUCTIVE SINGULARITIES IN THE STRUCTURAL TRANSITION SOLUTIONS IN VANDELVIRA’S WORK:

helping us to discern the harmonious intersection modern procedure whose renewing spirit remains
between theory and procedural praxis in one of consistently recognizable.
the most outstanding architects of the sixteenth In summary, the main findings derived from thiscentury. research may be stated as follows:
• Procedural experimentalism. The solutions
proposed, both by Andrés de Vandelvira in
his built work and by his son Alonso when
transcribing his father’s intellectual legacy
in the architectural treatise, demonstrate an
approach to design problems that departs
from what might be termed “functional
rationalism.” In this sense, Vandelvira’s
work goes well beyond resolving functional
and programmatic demands: it advances a
strong investigative dimension in which, as
explained, his broad command of metric and
projective geometry is mobilized to devise
genuinely ingenious solutions.
• Constructive formalization. The specific
cases in which rotation-generated vaults are
formalized, as may be verified in the drawings
presented, require substantial expertise

SINGULARIDADES GEOMÉTRICAS Y CONSTRUCTIVAS EN LAS SOLUCIONES DE TRANSICIÓN ESTRUCTURAL EN LA OBRA DE
VANDELVIRA: LA GUARDIA COMO CASO DE ESTUDIO

grandes avances propuestos por Vandelvira con el
desarrollo de estas soluciones estriba en resolver
los abovedamientos de manera diferente a la
común, para el caso que nos ocupa, transformando
las esferas en fragmentos de toroides o superficies
afines generadas por rotación.
• Optimización del modelo estructural. El despliegue
de este tipo de técnicas formales que, a simple vista
podrían etiquetarse como “filigranas geométricas”,
ponen de manifiesto el compromiso que el
arquitecto otorga al planteamiento estructural
de sus diseños. Al estudiar este sistema de
abovedamiento comprendemos que Vandelvira
mezcla en sus soluciones arquitectónicas varias
técnicas de estabilización mecánica. Las bóvedas
generadas por rotación, como hemos explicado,
fundamentan su lógica portante en el arranque
de éstas desde lienzos murales con una enorme
rigidez estructural, lo que permite el planteamiento
de diseños mucho más esbeltos y visualmente más
elegantes.
• Singularidad estilística. Es evidente que el
planteamiento de estas peculiares soluciones
técnicas, junto con otras consideraciones plásticas
o compositivas que también son recurrentes en
la obra de Vandelvira, nos permiten decir que
en su producción arquitectónica existe un sello
de autor que lo diferencia de otros maestros. El
valor del trabajo de Vandelvira es innegable, pero
cuando sumamos su legado al de otros maestros
contemporáneos como Siloé, Hernán Ruiz,
Machuca o Gil de Hontañón, pronto comprendemos
que su huella sobre el conocimiento científico y
técnico de la arquitectura del xvi es imborrable.
• Transferencia del conocimiento. Finalmente,
cabría reseñar el hecho de que, gracias a la labor
de revisión intelectual llevada a cabo por su hijo
Alonso, los avances que desarrolla Andrés de
Vandelvira han sido estudiados y analizados en
profundidad. El valor pedagógico del manuscrito
de Alonso de Vandelvira permitió, en su momento,
que la obra del arquitecto de Alcaraz impregnase
el pensamiento de sus colegas profesionales
mientras que, en la actualidad, nos permite abrir
fértiles vías de investigación que nos ayudarán a
preservar y valorar adecuadamente nuestro rico
patrimonio histórico-artístico.

THE GUADALAJARA CASINO BY ARCHITECT AURELIO BOTELLA ENRÍQUEZ:
THE BRIEF LIFE OF AN EXPONENT OF EXPRESSIONIST RATIONALISM

Antonio Miguel Trallero Sanz  a, Antonio Miguel Trallero Arroyo b
a Department of Architecture. Universidad de Alcalá. Spain. antonio.trallero@uah.es
b School of Architecture. Universidad de Alcalá. Spain. miguel.trallero@edu.uah.es

Abstract
Following the fire that destroyed the Guadalajara Casino on 14 September 1930, the Society
commissioned architect Aurelio Botella Enríquez to design a new building, the project for which was
drafted that same year. A few months later, he produced a revised proposal simplifying the programme,
which was ultimately the version that was built. This project constitutes the subject of the present
study. Through its analysis, the aim is to recover its memory and, above all, to highlight its importance
from an architectural point of view. This new building was destroyed in July 1936. It was a Rationalist
building with a marked Expressionist character, designed in 1930, the same year in which Luis Gutiérrez
Soto designed the Barceló Cinema, a building with which, despite the difference in scale, it shares
many similarities. Beyond the analogies in the façades and other formal solutions previously employed
by Gutiérrez Soto (Europa Cinema, 1928), particularly noteworthy is the use of the innovative diagonal
floor-plan model of the Barceló Cinema, the same model that Aurelio Botella Enríquez employed in the
Guadalajara Casino in the very same year as Gutiérrez Soto.

Keywords: diagonal floor plan; curved chamfer; Guadalajara Casino; Botella Enríquez; Gutiérrez Soto.

Resumen
Tras el incendio del Casino de Guadalajara, producido el 14 de septiembre de 1930, esta Sociedad
encargó al arquitecto Aurelio Botella Enriquez el proyecto de un nuevo edificio, que fue redactado
ese mismo año. Meses después diseñó una nueva propuesta simplificando el programa que fue
la que finalmente se ejecutó. Este proyecto es el objeto del presente estudio. Tras su estudio, se
pretende recuperar su memoria y, sobre todo, poner en valor su importancia desde un punto de vista
arquitectónico. Este nuevo edificio fue destruido en julio de 1936.
Se trataba de un edificio racionalista, con marcado carácter expresionista, diseñado en 1930, el mismo
año en que Luis Gutiérrez Soto proyectó el Cine Barceló, edificio con el que, a pesar de su diferencia
de tamaño, guarda muchas similitudes. Más allá de las analogías en fachada y otras solucionesValència
de formales que ya había empleado Gutiérrez Soto con anterioridad (cine Europa, 1928), destaca el uso
del innovador modelo de planta en diagonal del cine Barceló, el mismo modelo que, en el mismo año
que lo hace Gutiérrez Soto, lo utiliza Aurelio Botella Enriquez en el Casino de Guadalajara.Politècnica
Universitat Palabras clave: planta en diagonal; chaflán curvo; Casino Guadalajara; Botella Enriquez; Gutiérrez Soto.

TRALLERO SANZ, A.M. AND TRALLERO ARROYO, A.M.

\1. INTRODUCTION ﻿
The Sociedad Casino Principal de Guadalajara
was founded in 1869. It was a private association
which, like many others created in Spain from the
second half of the nineteenth century onwards,
was established for cultural and social purposes.
To fulfil these aims, spaces were required
where members could gather, read, play board
games, organise dances, social gatherings,
lectures, concerts, and other activities. In order
to accommodate these functions, new buildings
were constructed or existing ones adapted,
becoming from that moment onwards the centres
of social life in many cities.

1.1. THE GUADALAJARA CASINO
The first building that served as the headquarters
of the Guadalajara Main Casino was located on
the same site currently occupied by the present
building, at the corner of Calle Mayor, formerly
known as Fernando Palanca Street, and Calle del
Carmen, opposite Plaza del Jardinillo.

﻿ 2.1. APPROACH TO THE ARCHITECT AURELIO
BOTELLA ENRÍQUEZ
We know that this architect was born in 1900 and
obtained his degree in architecture in 1924. He
served as the municipal architect of Guadalajara
between 1928 and 1936, remaining professionally
active there until at least 1940 (Baldellou, 1989:
223).
Aurelio Botella Enríquez was a specialist in
healthcare architecture (Pieltain Álvarez-Arenas,
\2003) and collaborated with other specialists,
among them Martín José Marcide Odriozola,
the architect in charge of the Instituto Nacional
de Previsión, (https://www.urbipedia.org/hoja/
Mart%C3%ADn_Jos%C3%A9_Marcide).
Together with Ernesto Ripollés, Sebastián
Vilata, and Ambrosio Arroyo, he won first prize
in the competition for preliminary designs
of anti-tuberculosis sanatoriums (Ripollés,
Ernesto; Botella, Aurelio; Vilata, Sebastián;
Arroyo, Ambrosio, 1943: 122–144). He was also
responsible for the Alcohete Anti-Tuberculosis
Sanatorium, located near Guadalajara and

2.2. THE PROJECT FOR THE GUADALAJARA ﻿
CASINO
As previously indicated, only a few months after
the fire that destroyed the Guadalajara Casino
building, the Casino Society commissioned
architect Aurelio Botella Enríquez to design a
new building. This project is the subject of the
present study.
The Municipal Archive of Guadalajara preserves
both the original documentation, drafted in the
same year as the fire, that is, in 1930, and a
revised project dated 1931.
The building occupied the plot created after the
demolition of the remains left by the fire and the
setback of its façade along Calle Mayor (Alta), in
order to adapt it to the new alignment established

The principal spaces were grouped on a single ﻿
level, the Main Floor (Fig. 9), and the two central
circular spaces were reduced to one, serving the
dual function of Ballroom and Dining Room. The
semi-elliptical staircase, previously an important
interior element within the complex, was also
eliminated and replaced by a service staircase
connecting this level with the semi-basement
floor.
Another eliminated feature was the terrace
cinema, the flat roof being replaced by a pitched
Fig. 10. Project for the Guadalajara Casino. Section.roof concealed behind the parapets of the
\1931. Architect: Aurelio Botella Enríquez. AMG.façades.

﻿ The structure was designed with concrete foun-
dations from which walls rose—also of concrete
at the semi-basement level and of brickwork on
the main floor—with floors supported by dou-
ble-T metal beams. The main hall was covered by
a metal truss structure from which hung a vault
made of reed matting and plaster.
After the terrace was eliminated, the roof became
pitched and covered with flat ceramic tiles.
Externally, the façade was rendered, and its only
decorative features consisted of cantilevered el-
ements constructed using metal-framed panels.
For the construction of the building according to
the final proposal, planning permission was re-
quested from the City Council on 21 September
\1931. Later, on 18 August 1932, while the building
was already under construction, Aurelio Botella
designed a new entrance to the theatre by exFig. 9. Project for the Guadalajara Casino. Main Floor. tending the casino façade into the space between

Due to the location of the original “First Floor”
at semi-basement level, with openings set into
the building’s plinth, the elimination of one floor
did not substantially modify the building’s main
elevation (Fig. 11).
From a constructive point of view, this new building
also represented a radical departure from the
solutions characteristic of traditional architecture
and of the original construction. Modern materials
such as reinforced concrete, steel, and glass were
employed in the new building. Fig. 11. Project for the Guadalajara Casino. Main

\4. THE CASINO AFTER THE SPANISH CIVIL
WAR
After the Spanish Civil War, as a consequence of
the destruction of the Casino building, the Society
established its headquarters in the former Café
de la Amistad, a venue located opposite the ruins
of the Casino and which had remained closed
since the outbreak of the Civil War.
The main social celebrations were held in the
Teatro Casino, for which the seats in the stalls
were removed and the floor levelled.
In order to improve the facilities, in 1945 a new
project was commissioned from architects José Fig. 20. Reconstruction Project for the Social Building

EL CASINO DE GUADALAJARA DEL ARQUITECTO AURELIO BOTELLA ENRÍQUEZ: LA BREVE VIDA DE UN
EXPONENTE DEL RACIONALISMO EXPRESIONISTA

de él plateando una fachada con una composición
que seguía un planteamiento propio del eclecticismo
clasicista seguido en muchas de las actuaciones
llevadas a cabo en el primer tercio del siglo xx, en la
ciudad.
En el año 1949 se encargó un nuevo proyecto al
Arquitecto Antonio Labrada Chércoles, que fue el
que, con alguna modificación, fue ejecutado por el
arquitecto José María Rodríguez Garrido.
En cuanto al teatro, en el año 1946 abrió al público
con el nombre de Teatro Liceo hasta que en 1965 fue
demolido para construir sobre su solar un edificio del
Banco Castellano.

\5. CONCLUSIONES
El edificio del Casino de Guadalajara fue proyectado
por el Arquitecto Aurelio Botella Enríquez en el
año 1930. Se trata de una obra de tamaño no muy
grande que no llegó a construirse conforme al
proyecto inicialmente redactado sino según a un
segundo proyecto (1931) de este mismo arquitecto
que, manteniendo sus planteamientos y soluciones,
disminuyó considerablemente el programa, eliminando
una de sus plantas.
Este edificio fue destruido en el año 1936, motivo
por el cual no es suficientemente conocido y está
escasamente estudiado, a pesar de sus incuestionables
valores.
Se trata de un buen ejemplo racionalismo que, como
ocurre con la arquitectura de Luis Gutiérrez Soto,
previa a la Guerra Civil, muestra una clara influencia del
expresionismo alemán (Erich Mendelsohn) e incluso
del Art Decó, manifestada en sus fachadas e interiores,
pero lo que más llama la atención es la fecha en la que
se produjo, 1930. El año en el mismo año en el que
Gutiérrez Soto proyecta el cine Barceló incorporando
su planta en diagonal, Aurelio Botella la utiliza también
en el Casino de Guadalajara.

PORTRAITS OF ARCHITECTS: ANOTHER APPROACH TO THE STUDY OF THE
TECHNIQUE OF ARCHITECTURAL DRAWING AND ITS EVOLUTION BETWEEN THE
16TH AND 20TH CENTURIES

Federico Iborra Bernad
Departamento de Composición Arquitectónica, Universitat Politècnica de València, Spain. feibber@cpa.upv.es

Abstract
For centuries, it has been customary to portray architects with a pair of compasses, although they can
also be seen with plans or books. However, there are not many portraits showing architects actively
working with their instruments. Hence, the decision was made to prepare this study based on a selection
from the scarce located examples of such works, which can be analysed from a dual perspective. First,
we shall examine the drawing instruments depicted in each period and their method of use, looking
beyond the dominant museum-oriented approach in the related literature. We shall also briefly review
the biography of each figure, which may perhaps enable us to better understand the portrait, uncovering
some of the interests, complexes, and concerns of its protagonists.

Keywords: portrait; history of drawing; history of technology; architectural graphic expression; drawing
instruments.

Resumen
Durante siglos ha sido habitual representar a los arquitectos con un compás, aunque también se los
puede ver con planos o libros. Sin embargo, no hay muchos retratos donde se muestre a los arquitectos
trabajando con sus instrumentos. Por ello se decidió preparar el presente estudio a partir de una
selección entre los escasos ejemplos localizados de obras de este tipo, que se pueden analizar desde
una doble perspectiva. En primer lugar, estudiaremos los instrumentos de dibujo representados
en cada época y su modo de uso, más allá del enfoque museístico dominante en la bibliografía al
respecto. También revisaremos brevemente la biografía de cada personaje, lo que quizá nos permitirá
entender algo mejor el retrato, descubriendo algunos de los intereses, complejos e inquietudes de sus
protagonistas.

Palabras clave: retrato; historia del dibujo; historia de la técnica; expresión gráfica arquitectónica;
València instrumentos de dibujo.
de

Within the British world, it is worth highlighting counter Buckland’s actual artisanal background as

PORTRAITS OF ARCHITECTS: ANOTHER APPROACH TO THE STUDY OF THE TECHNIQUE OF ARCHITECTURAL DRAWING AND ITS EVOLUTION
BETWEEN THE 16TH AND 20TH CENTURIES

﻿ It should not escape notice that the architect
clutches a modern pencil in his right hand, an
instrument whose use is confirmed by Lequeu's
contemporary plate (1782), next to the quill pen
(Fig. 9). Conrad Gessner (1565: 104r-104v) was
the first to describe and depict a sort of wooden
propelling pencil, although by that time the idea
of encasing natural graphite bars in string or
grooved wood must have been widespread. This
latter solution prevailed, with a major production
hub developing around Nuremberg in the 17th
century, featuring specialised craftsmen like
Friedrich Staedtler (1662), although the first
industrial pencil manufactory would be Faber’s
(1761), also established near that town (Hambly,
1988: 65-66; Petroski, 2014: 79-84). Throughout
the 18th century they became cheaper as low-
quality leads were produced by mixing graphite
powder with sulphur (Petroski, 2014: 62-66),
which might perhaps explain their wider diffusion
in the second half of the century, even leading to
novel formats adapted to specific needs, such as
their use in compasses.25

refers to this particular type, characterised by ﻿
the pronounced separation between its legs, as
a "German-style" compass. In France, it was the
predominant compass type in the second half of
the 18th century and survived until the mid-19th
century, as demonstrated by its belated presence
in Buron's catalogue (1844: 96). Nevertheless, at
the time the portrait was painted, French architects
predominantly used straight compasses. It is
possible that this design held more currency in
Sweden, as it was even reproduced in some late
instrument cases, such as those produced by
Hadar Hallström from 1891 onwards.27

Similarly characteristic of this era are the wooden
folding rulers, inheritors of the sectors, which
endured well into the early decades of the 20th
century. Like the former, these rulers sported a
graduated scale on one edge that ran continuously
when fully open, but they removed the calculation
scales.30 In the specific case depicted, a slight
asymmetry is sensed in the metal mounting, which
indicates that it is a folding set square rather than
a simple ruler. This accounts for the absence of
other instruments for drafting perpendiculars31

PORTRAITS OF ARCHITECTS: ANOTHER APPROACH TO THE STUDY OF THE TECHNIQUE OF ARCHITECTURAL DRAWING AND ITS EVOLUTION
BETWEEN THE 16TH AND 20TH CENTURIES

\5. THE 20TH CENTURY: BETWEEN TRADITION ﻿
AND THE AVANT-GARDE
The custom of portraying architects with
compasses declined swiftly from the mid-19th
century onwards. The inception of photography
and the abandonment of the plain dividers as
a measuring tool, facilitated by the spread of
modern scale rulers, played a vital role. It is hence
original and striking to alight upon two portrayals
of architects wielding completely disparate
compasses in such late chronologies. We shall
see, however, that both enshrine a profound
message stretching far beyond preliminary
appearances.
Edward Guy Dawber (1861-1938) was a landscape
designer and one of the ultimate architects of
the Arts & Crafts movement. He specialised
in designing dwellings and minor vernacular-
style buildings. Rather than through his works,
he garnered tremendous prestige as a scholar
through his research into British vernacular
architecture.35 Between 1925 and 1927 he sat as
president of the RIBA, which awarded him the Fig. 14. Edward Guy Dawber (1861-1938), by William
Royal Gold Medal in 1928. A decade later, in 1936, Newenham Montague Orpen (1930). London, Royal

\6. CONCLUSIONS
﻿ As specified at the onset, there are few portraits
displaying architects actively engaged in
work; hence, breaking them down assists in
uncovering the evolution lying behind drawing
techniques. Yet in alternating scenarios, drawing
tools might embody part of a more subtle
undertone, spun through veiled symbolism.
Master Eudes de Montreuil, penned by André
Thevet, anachronistically works with drawing
tools of the 16th century, while the Dutch painter
Jacob Vennekool, steeped in the Baroque age,
brandishes the virga geometrica exactly like a
medieval architect. The cabinet-maker William
Buckland pretends to look like a grand builder,
whilst the builder Jacques-Denis Antoine yearns
to come off like an artist; concurrently, Josef Gerl
pays a muffled tribute to his departed father.
Nyström’s portrait ideally mirrors the burden of

greater dimensional stability, as the wood fibres run parallel to the interwar bracket.

en construcción que parece ser una puerta urbana o
un arco de triunfo, lo que se puede interpretar como
la metáfora del arquitecto constructor de una nueva
sociedad.

\6. CONCLUSIONES
Como se ha indicado al principio, no son muchos los
retratos que representan a arquitectos trabajando,
por lo que su análisis ayuda a entender la evolución
de las técnicas de dibujo. Pero, en algunos casos, los
instrumentos pueden formar parte de un mensaje más
sutil, expresado mediante simbolismos velados. El
maestro Eudes de Montreuil, representado por André
Thevet, usa anacrónicamente instrumentos de dibujo
del siglo xvi, mientras que el pintor holandés Jacob
Vennekool, en plena época barroca, sostiene la virga
geometrica como si fuera un arquitecto de época
medieval. El ebanista William Buckland finge ser un
gran constructor, mientras que el constructor JacquesDenis Antoine pretende parecer un artista, a la par
que Josef Gerl rinde un velado homenaje a su difunto
padre. El retrato de Nyström representa perfectamente
el peso de la tradición académica, incluyendo el uso
de un tipo de compás que ya se había abandonado
en el resto de Europa, mientras que el medievalismo
victoriano de Pugin se diseña con las herramientas
más avanzadas de su tiempo. Finalmente, los retratos
coetáneos de Dawber y Chiattone representarán dos
visiones del arte contrapuestas, la tradición renovada
y la vanguardia, que coexistieron en la arquitectura del
período de entreguerras.

APEGA, UPV
ISSN 2605-082X

360º HYBRIDIZATION AND VISUAL DIACHRONY: AN INCREMENTAL METHODOLOGY
FOR THE SPATIAL READING OF RUINS. THE CASE OF AMARO PARGO HOUSE

Iris López Armas  a, José Luis Saorín Pérez  b1, Dámari Melián Díaz b2, Jorge de la Torre Cantero  b3
a Construction Engineering Area, Universidad de La Laguna, Spain.  ilopezar@ull.edu.es
b Engineering Graphic Area, Universidad de La Laguna, Spain.
b1 jlsaorin@ull.edu.es, b2 dmeliand@ull.edu.es; b3 jcantero@ull.edu.es

Abstract
This research presents an innovative methodology for the diachronic interpretation of architectural
ruins through hybrid 360º environments. Focusing on the House of Amaro Pargo (Tenerife), a building in
an advanced state of decay, the study evaluates an incremental digital documentation process across
three stages: spherical photography, integration of multimedia data, and hybridization with 3D virtual
reconstructions. Using tools such as CloudPano and Twinmotion, a navigation system was developed
to allow real-time visual transitions between the current ruin and its historical hypothesis. The
effectiveness of the proposal was validated by a panel of 16 experts through comparative tests. Results
demonstrate that the combination of photorealistic recording and virtual synthesis significantly reduces
cognitive effort and improves spatial understanding of lost volumes. This work highlights the relevance
of hybrid immersive environments as essential tools for the graphic recovery and dissemination of
threatened domestic heritage.

Keywords: 360°virtual tours; architectural heritage in ruins; digital reconstruction; spatial understanding;
immersive environments.

This communicative potential also entails an ﻿
interpretive challenge: when real ruins and
reconstructions are alternated, some users
may mistake the restitution for a “true” state
if there is no minimal signaling of the model’s
interpretive character, an aspect identified as
good practice in heritage visualization (Denard,
2009). Complementarily, the literature on 360°
photo tours indicates that adding information
layers (floor plan, sketches, historical images, and
audiovisual resources) improves orientation and
perceived learning (Meier et al., 2024), although
in ruin scenarios the improvement also depends
on linking that information to a plausible spatial
restitution through an explicit present–past
comparison.
The case study selected for this work is Casa de
Amaro Pargo, in the hamlet of Machado (El Rosario,
Tenerife), a property listed as a Bien de Interés
Cultural and currently in ruins (Fig. 1). Its state of
conservation and the looting pressure associated
with the “treasure” legend have compromised its

ERRANT ARTWORKS AND PATRIMONY IN EVOLUTION: THE VIRTUAL
RECONSTRUCTION OF THE CRYPT IN SANTA MARIA DEI BIANCHI IN GUBBIO, ITALY

Alexia Charalambous
Dipartimento di Architettura, University of Florence, Italy. alexia.charalambous@unifi.it

Abstract
Every architecture and work of art is built in its time under good hopes and goodwill, and then time
and events can bring incredible transformations. Tracking and telling these stories may require a multi-
layered sequence of operations: accurately documenting the state of the building, reading/interpreting
the transformation, laying foundations for communication and dissemination of complex content, and
then leaving a shareable record and products that can be used for future stewardship and protection
are all tasks and goals that any digital intervention should consider. The case study presented here is
based on the small church of SantaMariadeiBianchi in Gubbio, Italy, an architecture that originated
in the Middle Ages and has undergone many changes over time. It is a complex mix of changes and
events that need to be communicated to visitors in an appropriate way, and the need to define a proper
strategy for planning the next steps in the management of this architectural and artistic heritage. By
using digital surveying with lasergrammetry and photogrammetry, integrating the creation of virtual
immersive environments and applying consolidated procedures for digital reconstruction, the result will
define a system based on a virtual tour and precise content that will help the visitor understand the
articulated history of the site and manage the entire architectural and artistic system.

Keywords: digital survey; virtual reality; digital reconstruction.

GRAPHIC DOCUMENTATION AND DIGITAL CHARACTERIZATION OF THE LANTERNS
OF THE ROYAL TOBACCO FACTORY IN SEVILLE: FROM LASER SCANNING AND
PHOTOGRAMMETRY TO HBIM MODELS

María del Rosario Chaza Chimeno a , Fernando Rico Delgado  b1, María Dolores Rincón Millán  b2,
Rafael Esteve Pardal  c
a Departamento de Diseño Gráfico e Ingeniería de la Edificación, Universidad de Sevilla, España. chaza@us.es
b Departamento de Diseño Gráfico e Ingeniería de la Construcción, Universidad de Sevilla, España.
b1 fricodel@us.es, b2 lola_rincon@us.es
c Departamento de Ingeniería Gráfica, Universidad de Sevilla, España. resteve@us.es

Abstract
The aim of this research is to make a comparison of the results obtained when carrying out graphic
surveys using different systems, with the aim of obtaining models which, in addition to graphically
documenting the current state of elements of undoubted architectural value, facilitate their use in
heritage intervention. To this end, the skylights on the roof of the former Royal Tobacco Factory of Seville,
the current headquarters of the Rectorate of the University, one of the most emblematic buildings in
the city due to its heritage and architectural value, have been taken as study elements, with the aim of
highlighting their importance, giving impetus to their recovery and conservation.
The aim is to document and highlight these elements using traditional drawing techniques combined
with modern methods such, 3D scanning or photogrammetry, to finally obtain a 3D virtual model
that, when taken to the BIM environment, generates an integral model that facilitates the analysis of
possible conservation works. The current trend is moving towards “smart heritage facility management”
(Hou et al., 2020). Integrating HBIM with digital twins enables a transition from reactive restoration to
proactive and predictive maintenance (Hartwell et al.,2025). One of the seven original skylights, dating
from the mid-18th century, are taken as the object of study.

Keywords: historical heritage; digital survey; BIM; 3D scanning; photogrammetry; lanterns.

Resumen
El objetivo de esta investigación es comparar los resultados obtenidos al realizar levantamientos gráficos
utilizando diferentes sistemas, con el fin de elaborar modelos que, además de documentar gráficamente
el estado actual de elementos de indudable valor arquitectónico, faciliten su uso en la conservación delValència
de patrimonio. Con este fin, se han seleccionado como objeto de estudio las claraboyas del tejado de la
antigua Fábrica Real de Tabaco de Sevilla —actualmente sede del Rectorado de la Universidad y uno de
los edificios más emblemáticos de la ciudad por su valor patrimonial y arquitectónico—, con el objetivoPolitècnica de poner de relieve su importancia y promover su restauración y conservación.
Universitat

CHAZA CHIMENO, M. DEL R., RICO DELGADO, F., RINCÓN MILLÁN, M. D., AND ESTEVE PARDAL, R.

Se propone documentar y poner en valor estos elementos mediante las técnicas de dibujo tradicionales
alternados con métodos actuales como el escaneo 3D o la fotogrametría, para obtener en última instancia
un modelo virtual 3D que, al integrarse en el entorno BIM, genere un modelo completo que facilite el
análisis de posibles obras de conservación. La tendencia actual se orienta hacia la «gestión inteligente
de las instalaciones patrimoniales» (Hou et al., 2020). La integración de HBIM con gemelos digitales
permite pasar de una restauración reactiva a un mantenimiento proactivo y predictivo (Hartwell et al.,
2025). Se toma como objeto de estudio una de las siete claraboyas originales, que datan de mediados
del siglo xviii.

Palabras clave: patrimonio histórico; catalogación; BIM; escáner 3D; fotogrametría; linternas

\2. METHODOLOGY
To achieve this objective, we propose comparing
traditional surveying techniques with new
methods, such as those based on 3D laser
scanning or photogrammetry, to ultimately
obtain a virtual model. When integrated into
a BIM environment, this model generates a
comprehensive representation that facilitates
analysis during restoration works, subsequent
preventive maintenance tasks, management,
and the promotion of existing heritage. The use,
therefore, of new technologies such as 3D laser
scanning in heritage conservation and restoration
work (Rodríguez-Moreno et al., 2018), following
established protocols (Simeone et al., 2019),
is of great assistance in carrying out the actual
Fig. 2. L7 lantern from the Royal Tobacco Factory in geometric survey and volumetric analysis of the
Seville. Photography by the authors. architectural element through the generated
point cloud.
This point cloud generates a BIM model (Volk et al.,
circles of varying diameters—is constructed of 2014), in which the elements to be replaced are
bricks, finished with a mortar render and topped virtually identified and defined (McArthur, 2015)
with glazed ceramic tiles. and enables their use in applications for multimedia
Constructed mostly of ceramic pieces, they devices and the subsequent realistic display of the
feature glazed ceramic finishes in shades of resulting models (Natephra et al, 2017).
indigo.
With a similar aesthetic pattern, they feature ﻿ceramic crowning elements in the form of ornate
vases of the same proportions.
We will now examine one of the most characteristic
lanterns, designated L7 (Fig. 2.), located in the
south façade bay.
Its interior is finished with a continuous plaster
coating, accompanied by beautiful plasterwork
ornamentation; these decorative elements allude
to 17th-century Sevillian Baroque plasterwork,
with glazed ceramic tiles and ceramic vases
crowning the dome.
It should be noted that the new Baroque style
appeared in Seville from the 1650s onwards,
developing in three highly representative
buildings: the Tabernacle of the Cathedral and
the churches of the Hospital de la Santa Caridad Fig. 3. Sketch, measurements and cost estimate for

The use of supervoxels to improve point cloud ﻿
segmentation in historic buildings with non-
uniform densities, would help to better define
architectural features (Salamanca et al., 2024).
The 3D scanning techniques and methods
discussed in the existing scientific and industrial
literature (Daneshmand et al, 2018) offer a wide
range of possibilities; consequently, some of
these have been implemented in this study. The
planimetric survey of the lanterns has focused on
three methods: Traditional, Photogrammetric and
Laser Scanning, from which the BIM methodology
is applied to obtain the virtual 3D model.
The integration of TLS (Terrestrial Laser
Scanning) and photogrammetry is currently the
fastest-growing area of research for maximizing
accuracy and spatial coverage (Yang et al., 2020).

This photogrammetry method is widely used in
the processing of images captured by unmanned ﻿
aerial vehicles (UAVs) (Tahar, 2013). The process
continues by creating so-called ‘masks’ on
the photographs, which serve to optimize the
software’s processing of the images. Created
manually, they are essential for hiding certain
areas and excluding them from the processing.
In other words, we delineate the images, thereby
removing all elements that do not contribute
information to the survey, such as the sky, the
ground, surrounding vegetation, and other objects
that are not part of the three-dimensional model Fig. 6. Point cloud, dense mesh and textured mesh.

2.3 3D LASER SCANNING ﻿
The use of 3D laser scanners in architectural
surveys of heritage buildings is widely documented
and practised (Zlot, 2014; Tomljenovic et al.,
2015; Ebrahim, 2016), and provides us with a
point cloud that can be exported as a reference
precise geometry, into CAD/BIM software, where
we can create cross-sections to develop a solid
model that matches, as closely as possible, the
geometry of the analyzed element (Hichri et al.,
2013).
